# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
````

PAGE 1 Description of Dataset and Learning Task words 483
0 h 1.1 Source, scope and structure of the records
1 p BattLeDIM (Battle of the Leakage Detection and Isolation Methods) is an open, simulated drinking-water benchmark representing L-Town, inspired by Cyprus. Originally designed for leak detection/localization, its hydraulic time series also support supervised pressure forecasting [1,2]. Zenodo supplies SCADA, leak annotations, sensor configuration and EPANET models. These are simulated observations, not field measurements.
2 p The release spans 2018-2019 at five-minute intervals: 105,120 records per year, 210,240 total, and 119 numerical measurement channels per timestamp. The nominal model has 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and three PRVs. Its parameters differ from the published generator model, so it provides structural context rather than exact hydraulic ground truth [1].
3 table [['Recorded quantity', 'Channels', 'Type / physical unit', 'Inter
4 p Structural checks of all eight annual SCADA tables found aligned, unique, regularly spaced timestamps and no missing values [25]. The benchmark assumes error-free sensing, no transmission delay or lost packets, and two-decimal rounding. Each pressure reading averages the preceding five minutes; each AMR aggregates consumption of multiple users assigned to its junction [2, Sensors and Telemetry]. Adjacent records are dependent; leak annotations are auxiliary information and never predictor inputs.
5 h 1.2 A concrete supervised learning task
6 p The task is single-output supervised regression: y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min), three sampling steps ahead, in metres. This recorded label represents the five-minute average ending at u+15, not instantaneous pressure. Shifting the n1 series supplies labels without manual annotation. The use case is short-term pressure monitoring at a remote measured junction; there are no target classes.
7 p Use only information available by u: n1/n4/n31 pressures, T1 level, PUMP_1 flow and AMR_n1 demand, with past histories and known calendar inputs. Future labels never enter X. Six measured channels yield more than six feature columns; Section 4 fixes the planned histories and comparison budgets.
8 h 1.3 A local supply boundary that motivates the study

0 h 3.1 Applying the rubric to original-source research
1 p This section addresses the rubric criterion titled "Kaggle Contributions." BattLeDIM/L-Town is obtained directly from its organizers, so the relevant contributors are research authors and competition developers publishing papers, team reports and code repositories, rather than Kaggle notebook authors [1,2]. We retain the same assessment questions: what was learned, how data and models were prepared, and what performance was achieved under which evaluation conditions.
2 p The targeted search and inventory in Section 2 identify a broader corpus. For detailed exposition we prioritize five learning contributions that connect directly to pressure prediction, local pressure/flow regression or sparse pressure estimation. A sixth, engineering-based diagnostic study is included as supporting context because it already uses the Area C tank/pump/AMR balance. These sources offer distinct mechanisms and documented outcomes; they are not six independent solutions to our exact n1(+15) task.
3 table
4 image
5 p <b>How results are interpreted.</b> MAE/RMSE in metres assess pressure/head error; MAPE reports relative error and NSE measures agreement relative to a mean-value reference. TP/FP/FN, location tolerance and economic score evaluate leak detection/localization. Different data origins, node sets, time horizons and scoring rules prevent a single accuracy ranking. All results below are the authors' published outcomes; source/code inspection does not constitute reproduction of training.

PAGE 6 WORDS 699
0 h 3.2 ChebNet: forecasting and reconstruction as complementary diagnostic models
1 p <b>Task and preparation.</b> Gardarsson, Boem and Toni [3] train a predictor of next-step nodal pressure and a reconstructor of the current pressure field. Both learn from pressure generated by the nominal hydraulic model; sparse pressure observations and topology are inputs, while full simulated nodal pressures supply labels. Their difference is used to diagnose leaks in official SCADA, including the 2019 evaluation series. Graph-weight/scaling choices, observed-node handling and historical windows are explored; residual plots examine how leakage changes the learned relationships.
2 p <b>Model and time alignment.</b> Four Chebyshev graph-convolution layers propagate pressure features through the pipe graph. Hidden polynomial orders [240,120,20] and filter sizes [120,60,30], SiLU hidden activations, sigmoid output and Adam/weight decay define the inspected architecture [3,6]. History is stacked as input channels. T=3 is selected from {1,2,3,6,12,24}; at five-minute sampling it is a 15-minute history. The pinned loader ends X at row i-1 and labels row i: the forecast lead is +5 minutes, not +15.
3 p <b>Performance and significance.</b> Predictor-minus-reconstructor residuals are mapped from nodes to edges and analyzed with seven-day moving statistics, alpha=1 and a one-alarm-per-day/six-hop neighborhood filter. The article reports 19 TP, 138 FP, 4 FN, TPR=82.61% and EUR326,521 for leakage diagnosis (Table 1, pp.5-6). A match requires an alarm during the leak lifespan and within 300 m. This demonstrates useful sparse graph inference, but also a substantial false-alarm burden; it is not a reported pressure MAE for n1(+15).
4 p <b>Code and transfer to our study.</b> <link href="https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/blob/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8/04%20Leakage%20Detection/utils/data_loader.py#L285-L290">Window/label construction</link> and <link href="https://github.com/GardarGardarsson/GSP_for_Leak_Detection/blob/864a99041af6331375946a8fad3a71c633108eb8/04%20Leakage%20Detection/predict.py#L120-L131">preparation and chronological 80/20 split</link> are directly inspectable [6]. Scaling precedes that split in the selected script; our protocol fits transforms on training only. A nominal healthy reference for diagnosis and a forecast intended to follow recorded pressure during a leak have different target meanings.
5 h 3.3 SAM-kNN: adaptive local virtual pressure sensors
6 p <b>Task, data and preparation.</b> Jakob et al. [4] simulate ten three-month WNTR scenarios on modified L-Town Area A (661 nodes, 766 links, 29 pressure sensors), every five minutes: five single leaks and five sensor faults. These are generated scenarios, not original 2019 SCADA. The paper (Sec.5.1) describes averaging a window of four rows; the pinned <link href="https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/LeakDbScenarioPreprocessing.py#L19-L30">preprocessor</link> instead averages three past rows and labels the fourth. Our +5-minute interpretation follows that inspected code; this paper/code difference is preserved. Each virtual sensor excludes its own target channel from the inputs.
7 p <b>Model and evaluation.</b> Short-term, long-term and combined memories generate competing kNN predictions; tracked errors select a memory, with cleaning/compression to retain useful older concepts while adapting. The pinned experiment skips the first 100 raw samples, initializes from 3,000 processed rows and then predicts batches of 200 before updating. Comparators are online kNN (k=5, window=500) and linear regression. Pressure discrepancies trigger alarms; the published leak results are:
8 table
9 p <b>Interpretation and code.</b> Table 1 (p.10) counts whether the single event was detected for TP/FN, but counts every false alarm for FP. Thus the large FP reduction is an anomaly-detection result, not pressure MAE or row-wise classification accuracy. <link href="https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/SAM_KNN_Regression.py">Adaptive memories</link> and <link href="https://github.com/andreArtelt/SAM-kNN-Regressor_OnlineLearning_WDNs/blob/9116ec99bf9f053e35401fe3e388062e8b979c54/Implementation/experiments.py">stream ordering/configuration</link> support the mechanism [7]; scenario data are not supplied in the repository. For our +15 task, online updates must wait until each future label becomes observable. Adaptation and next-step pressure prediction already have clear precedents.

PAGE 7 WORDS 594
0 h 3.4 AGN: multistep future heads with graph attention and nodal attributes
1 p <b>Task and interpretation.</b> Zou et al. [11] describe demand-state forecasting "from a pressure perspective." The method forecasts future nodal heads (Eq.10; Fig.3), with pressure histories, node heights and node types as inputs. At a fixed node elevation, pressure head is hydraulic head minus elevation. This makes it a genuine temporal pressure-related antecedent, while preserving the paper's distinction between measured pressure inputs and future-head outputs.
2 p <b>Data exploration and preparation.</b> The authors plot elevation, node-use types and annual consumption patterns (Fig.2). They randomly assign 104 residential/commercial/industrial demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes and generate one year at five-minute resolution. An added pumping-station node yields a 783-node evaluation. These modified-network sequences are split chronologically 70/20/10; they are not the original recorded 2018/2019 SCADA outcomes. Pump/PRV connections receive special adjacency weights (300 and 1, respectively), while other edges use a hydraulic-proximity weighting derived from pipe properties (Secs.2.1,3.1).
3 p <b>Learning model and training preparation.</b> AGN extends gated graph/GRU propagation. Weighted neighbors exchange local states; self-attention supplies wider spatial dependencies; external attention fuses pressure states with height and use-type features to produce future heads (Eq.10). Cascade masking is a training strategy, not an output layer: sensor inputs/features are randomly zeroed, with the masked proportion progressively reduced (Sec.2.3). The comparison uses GGNN and graph-head reconstruction followed by GRU (GHR+GRU). Hidden dimension is 12, batch size 32, optimizer Adam; AGN/GGNN have three recurrent layers, initial learning rate 0.001 and epoch decay factor 0.8 (Sec.3.2) [11].
4 p <b>Forecast construction and measured performance.</b> History length equals forecast-window length: 12,24,48 or 96 five-minute steps. The windows end at +60,+120,+240 or +480 minutes and include all intervening steps. In particular, the third output is +15 minutes; selecting that horizon alone cannot establish novelty. Table 1 reports MAE and MAPE aggregated over forecast steps and nodes within elevation regions:
5 table
6 p <b>Significance and limits.</b> The method combines temporal forecasting with sparse-to-global inference and provides feature-masking and layer-depth analyses. Its regional results also expose a serious low-elevation failure: good accuracy is not uniform across the network. Longer windows change both the information supplied and the forecast target set, so Table 1 is not a controlled curve of error versus lead with fixed history. It does not report the isolated n1(+15) metric needed for our experiment.
7 p <b>Code and reproducibility.</b> The <link href="https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S259012302502969X">primary article</link> provides equations, settings and results; data are available on request. The article links inspected in our earlier full-text review supplied no repository hyperlink, despite its conclusion calling the model open-source. No inspected AGN implementation is linked here, and code nonexistence is not asserted. This revision rechecks Eq.10, masking and Table 1 in retained indexed publisher text [25]. Training was not reproduced. The authors acknowledge tuning leakage as a limitation; our protocol freezes choices before final assessment.

PAGE 8 WORDS 642
0 h 3.5 LILA: a regression reference followed by change detection and hydraulic localization
1 p <b>Task, analysis and preparation.</b> Daniel et al. [8] seek leak events and locations rather than future pressure. They examine correlated pressure behavior, select reference periods and sensor combinations, and fit relations expected within the same hydraulic condition. The publication derives pressure/flow reference models with squared-flow terms; the pinned <link href="https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA/blob/b488a60db1974b6f5b7887ce1cf5d31dff3dad68/_utils/BattLeDIM_utils.py#L204-L251">implementation</link> fits LinearRegression using another sensor's current pressure and raw PUMP_1 flow. This version-specific difference must be preserved, not silently equated.
2 p <b>Model.</b> Pressure-reference residuals form a most-affected-sensor trajectory. CUSUM accumulates changes, using variability-related thresholds, to identify a change point; calibrated hydraulic pressure-drop signatures and optimization identify candidate pipes. Re-estimation across states helps handle overlapping leaks. Manually selected reference windows and ex-post event annotations support the published analysis, so a prospective reproduction must verify what would have been known at each time.
3 p <b>Results.</b> The journal evaluation includes 19 leaks starting in 2019, excluding four inherited from 2018. Table 1 uses a 300 m localization tolerance and detection allowed during the leak lifespan:
4 table
5 p <b>Significance and boundary.</b> All 19 events were identified, but two localizations fell outside 300 m and count as FP. Recall=1 therefore does not mean every location was accepted. The separate result of locating all events within 374 m uses up to 41 days of allowed delay; it is not a rapid-warning guarantee. <link href="https://github.com/SWN-group-at-TU-Berlin/LILA/blob/b488a60db1974b6f5b7887ce1cf5d31dff3dad68/_utils/BattLeDIM_utils.py#L368-L407">CUSUM code</link> and reference fitting [9] make LILA an inspectable regression/change-detection precedent. Its score cannot be ranked against ChebNet's different event population or used as a pressure-forecast MAE.
6 h 3.6 GATRes: pressure reconstruction with masked training and distribution-aware testing
7 p <b>Task and preparation.</b> Truong et al. [10] reconstruct current pressures at unobserved junctions from sparse same-time pressures. Hydraulic simulations vary operating parameters; random sensor masks expose the model to changing observation layouts. Masked inputs are zero-filled and loss is computed at masked positions. For L-Town, 10,000 generated snapshots are split 60/20/20. A separate one-week demand-pattern series sampled every five minutes provides the temporal-pattern test (Sec.6.4). Sequential testing still processes each snapshot independently: there is no future-pressure target.
8 p <b>Model and EDA.</b> Projected pressures pass through graph-attention blocks with residual links and mean aggregation, then a linear pressure output. MSE is evaluated at masked nodes. For the benchmark comparison, Adam trains for at most 2,000 epochs; GATRes/mGCN stop after 250 epochs without a validation improvement of at least 1e-6, while GraphConvWat uses 50 (Sec.6.4). Density plots contrast generated-snapshot and demand-pattern distributions, showing why replica-distribution testing can be optimistic. These preparation and evaluation choices matter alongside the architecture.
9 p <b>Performance and code.</b> Table 10 reports L-Town MAE = 0.9501 +/- 0.0086 m for GATRes-small, versus 1.5928 +/- 0.0050 for mGCN and 3.4505 +/- 0.0129 for GraphConvWat; mean and standard deviation are over ten seeded runs. GATRes also reports MAPE=0.0157 +/- 0.0002 as a ratio and NSE=0.9000 +/- 0.0000. The calculated MAE reduction versus mGCN is 40.35%, within that experiment. <link href="https://github.com/DiTEC-project/gnn-pressure-estimation/blob/bb287ed2ae091b29fa19f2417406756b57a45a1f/gnn_pressure_estimation/GraphModels.py#L452-L495">Attention/residual blocks</link> and <link href="https://github.com/DiTEC-project/gnn-pressure-estimation/blob/bb287ed2ae091b29fa19f2417406756b57a45a1f/gnn_pressure_estimation/train.py">masked training</link> are inspectable [20]. These are generated-data reconstruction results, not accuracy on observed n1(+15); they motivate our attention to sensor coverage and evaluation distribution.

PAGE 9 WORDS 545
0 h 3.7 Supporting engineering contribution: EILF and the Area C storage balance
1 p <b>Why include it.</b> Wang et al. [17] provide the closest physical-input overlap with our proposal. EILF (estimation-identification-localization framework) is a statistical/hydraulic diagnostic framework, not a trained future-pressure regressor. It is reviewed separately from the five learning models so that data decomposition is not mislabeled as a supervised forecast.
2 p <b>Task, preparation and model.</b> The journal study uses labeled 2018 data for development and 2019 for testing. It treats Area C separately and combines Areas A/B because B has no separate inlet-flow meter. In C, pump inflow minus change in tank storage estimates sector inflow; subtracting aggregate AMR demand yields a diagnostic residual. The authors observe level-quantization inconsistencies and compute C inflow/demand over 30-minute intervals. For A/B, empirical mode decomposition (EMD) extracts a seasonal consumption component from C, combined with weekly demand patterns to estimate normal flow.
3 p Additive seasonal-trend decomposition using LOESS (STL) separates pressure trend, periodic behavior and residuals. Multiscale differences of flow residuals support three-sigma statistical process control and sliding-window incipient-leak assessment. Weekly pressure differences then enter a differential-search hydraulic localization procedure. The 2020 conference proposal [13] mentions VAR, whereas the journal formulation [17] uses STL; these versions must not be treated as one fully specified future-pressure model.
4 p <b>Performance and availability.</b> The journal conclusion (p.11) reports 16 detected/localized cases in 2019, TPR=47.83%, FP=5 and EUR167,981. The count of 16 and TPR describe the authors' reported diagnostic results; the conclusion does not supply a common denominator reconciling them. We therefore do not treat 16 as 16 accepted true positives or derive a new recall. These are not pressure-forecast errors. Code is available from the corresponding author on request (Data Availability, p.11); no public repository is supplied. The conference proposal is linked separately in [13]. No request was sent.
5 p <b>Consequence for our study.</b> Combining T1, pump flow and AMR information, using a tank balance and decomposing pressure are already prior work. Our candidate contribution must concern how past available information is used for observed n1(+15) and how its added value is evaluated. Any prospective use of EMD/STL or windowed balances must also check time availability; a retrospective decomposition is not automatically an online prediction.
6 h 3.8 What transfers, and what must be compared again
7 table
8 p The detailed rubric audit accompanies this section [21]: task, data/preparation, model, metric/result and code/access evidence are recorded for every case. Published values are retained in their own units and evaluation populations. We have inspected sources and selected code; we have not trained these models or reproduced their reported scores.

PAGE 10 WORDS 446
0 h 4.1 Contribution statement and the gap it addresses
1 p This section applies "Your Anticipated Contributions" to original-source BattLeDIM research rather than Kaggle notebooks. We propose a reproducible study of recorded n1 pressure at +15 minutes using only six measured signals: n1/n4/n31, T1, PUMP_1 and AMR_n1, plus causal histories and calendar variables. The question is whether storage-informed preparation improves a compact forecaster beyond pressure persistence and matched raw-history models. A secondary experiment asks how explicitly representing reading age changes performance under synthetic auxiliary delays [22].
2 p <b>What is already established.</b> ChebNet and SAM-kNN predict future pressures; AGN produces multistep heads including +15; LILA exploits pressure/flow relationships; GATRes studies sparse reconstruction and distribution shifts. EILF already combines tank level, pump flow and AMRs through storage balance [3-11,17,20]. Thus the horizon, selected node, Random Forest, increment target and balance equation are not individually claimed as inventions. Our reviewed sources do not report the matched six-signal, original-SCADA experiment specified here; this is a bounded evidence gap, not proof of worldwide priority.
3 table
4 h 4.2 Scope and feasible implementation
5 p The main experiment uses the original synchronized SCADA, without new hydraulic simulations or extra sensors. Random Forest is the main nonlinear learner because it can test nonlinear effects and interactions in a tabular six-signal design; Ridge supplies an interpretable linear benchmark [23,24]. Graph-network replication, online adaptive memories, an AMR-wide search and distance-based peer ranking are outside this initial contribution. Both peer pressures remain candidates; their value will be measured rather than assumed from pipe distance.
6 p The original-source setting changes attribution and reproducibility: organizer data, primary papers and pinned repositories replace Kaggle notebooks. Proposed code and experiments are our future deliverables, distinct from published results. Our label follows recorded pressure, including operating/leak conditions present in the benchmark; it is not a nominal healthy-pressure reference for detecting leaks. No learning model has been fitted for this report. The proposal targets a review-bounded empirical extension; benefits must meet the tests below.

PAGE 11 WORDS 512
0 h 4.3 Preparation: a supervised table whose timing can be checked
1 p For each five-minute forecast origin u, set y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min). In the primary synchronized experiment, construct 12 readings per selected channel at lags 0,5,...,55 minutes (72 numerical history columns). Add sine/cosine of time of day and seven weekday indicators (nine known calendar columns). Fix this history for all representation comparisons: a storage feature must not silently receive a longer lookback. These are 81 base columns derived from the same six measured channels, not 81 extra sensors.
2 p Align the four official tables by unique timestamp; verify spacing, units, duplicates and missingness before creating labels. Retain source time and forecast origin for every input. Never interpolate using future readings, fill a missing response or use leakage annotations as X. Fit Ridge scaling on training rows only; Random Forest requires no standardization. The complete synchronized data need no imputation; if an availability window cannot be built, exclude that origin from all paired variants and report its count.
3 p For the secondary synthetic-delay experiment, a reading sampled at t arrives at a_s(t)=t+delta_s(t). At origin u, use the received reading with the greatest source timestamp, tau_s(u)=max{t: a_s(t)<=u}; its age is u-tau_s(u). An older late packet cannot overwrite a newer cached sample. A history entry at u-5j is the cache state as it stood at that earlier origin; later arrivals never retroactively revise it. The target label remains the original recorded n1(+15), not a shifted delayed target.
4 h 4.4 Storage features and their physical limits
5 p Let L be T1 level, A=pi(16 m)^2/4 the nominal tank area, and w in {15,30} minutes. The storage block S has four features: L(v)-L(v-w) and the following estimated outlet-flow proxy for each w. The pump mean uses the w/5 right-end readings in (v-w,v], in m3/h:
6 math
7 p For each window choose the freshest endpoint v<=u for which both level endpoints and every required pump reading have arrived. This completeness check matters when packets arrive out of order. Store anchor age u-v. In synchronized data v=u. The balance is inherited from EILF [17]; nominal geometry, rounded level and discrete averaging make it a proxy, not measured sector outflow or total demand. Do not silently clip negative values or subtract all 82 AMRs; the AMR candidate remains only AMR_n1.
8 p <b>Why the comparison needs care.</b> With complete histories, these tank features are algebraic transformations of the raw level/flow inputs, not new measurements. For Ridge, linear changes and balance features remain within the raw linear feature span; any change may reflect scaling or regularization. The main representation comparison therefore uses the same Random Forest learner, where an engineered coordinate can change the finite tree fit. Ridge is retained as a redundancy/control check, not evidence of newly created physical information.

PAGE 12 WORDS 464
0 h 4.5 Supporting EDA: a hypothesis, not fitted-model performance
1 p The daily profiles and correlation matrix in Section 1 remain unchanged. It relates backward storage features to the future pressure increment, rather than only to the future pressure level. Source hashes and reproducible calculations accompany the protocol [22].

3 h 4.6 Learning models and matched representation tests
4 math
5 p n1 stays current in the primary and secondary experiments, so persistence predicts r(u). Fit Random Forest to the increment; also fit a direct-pressure Random Forest with the identical inputs and tuning budget to isolate the effect of target parameterization. Ridge is trained on the increment with train-only scaling. Own-n1-history plus calendar models and persistence are essential baselines: strong persistence means a low absolute error alone would not demonstrate a contribution.
6 p <b>E1: main synchronized comparison.</b> Compare Random Forest on all six raw histories/calendar (F00) with the same learner plus the four storage features (FS). Use identical origins, histories, targets, split and hyperparameter budget. Separately add signal blocks to own n1/calendar in the order T1, PUMP_1, peers n4/n31, AMR_n1, then remove one block at a time from the full raw model. This distinguishes a useful measurement block from a useful engineered representation; the order is not a claim of physical priority.
7 p <b>Model mechanism and budget.</b> Random Forest averages 200 regression trees fitted on bootstrap samples, with squared-error splits; depth and leaf size control complexity. Use max_features=1.0, seed 42 and six configurations: depth {6,10,14} crossed with minimum leaf size {10,40}. Ridge minimizes squared residuals plus alpha times the squared coefficient norm, with alpha {0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000}. Select by October-December 2018 MAE. Keep the primary history at 12 samples; later exploratory changes cannot be chosen from 2019 results [23,24].

PAGE 13 WORDS 575
0 h 4.7 Secondary extension: unequal delays with a learnable age signal
1 p SCADA provides sampling timestamps, not observed receipt times. This is a synthetic robustness experiment. Keep n1 current; for each of n4, n31, T1, PUMP_1 and AMR_n1 draw an independent delay per source day in {0,5,...,L} minutes, independently of pressures and leak labels. Unequal, time-varying delays make reading age vary; a fixed age column in each scenario could not establish a learned age effect. Use the as-of cache construction in Section 4.3, including source-time order and complete balance windows.
2 p Train delayed variants on 2018 schedules capped at 15 min (training seed 17; validation 19). All four receive the same cached histories plus the exact raw operands used in S: two level endpoints and w/5 pump samples for each w, adding 13 columns in this secondary experiment. This prevents the storage variant from receiving extra historical readings. Age block A adds five current auxiliary ages and two balance-anchor ages, identically available in FA/FSA. Freeze models, then assess caps 0/5/10/15 with seeds 23/29/31 and coupled draws. Synchronous and delayed training are separate experiments [25].
3 table
4 h 4.8 Temporal assessment and what counts as an improvement
5 p Fit January-September 2018 and select settings on October-December 2018. Purge origins whose +15 labels cross the boundary; carrying earlier predictor history across the boundary is legitimate. Use a common initial 75-minute warm-up, covering the 55-minute history plus maximum 15-minute delay. After selection, refit each chosen model on all eligible 2018 origins and freeze code, data hashes, feature lists, seeds and settings before the new 2019 experiment. Because we have already inspected this public benchmark, call 2019 a final temporal assessment, not an untouched blind test.
6 p <b>Measures and decisions.</b> Report MAE (primary) and RMSE in metres, plus skill=1-MAE_model/MAE_persistence. For E1, the primary paired contrast is MAE_FS-MAE_F00; an accuracy claim requires a negative contrast with a 95% block-bootstrap interval below zero and better MAE than persistence and own-history models. For E2, average MAE across nonzero caps and the three schedules; the age-specific primary contrast is FSA-FS. Also report FSA-FA, FSA-F00 and each cap separately. Average paired origin losses over schedules before resampling; schedules are not independent extra years.
7 p Use 2,000 moving-block resamples of paired losses with seven-day blocks; test one-/fourteen-day sensitivity. Report counts and MAE/RMSE for filling, draining and near-flat states, using received backward T1 change over 30 min (>0.01/<-0.01/otherwise, m; round numerical differences before thresholding). These are descriptive benchmark intervals, not field-population guarantees. Report RMSE tradeoffs, null/negative results and excluded origins. Operating-state association is not causal attribution.
8 p <b>Expected contribution and completion criterion.</b> Deliver the supervised-table builder with timing assertions, reproducible storage/increment models, and a frozen experiment manifest plus paired ablation results. The anticipated extension is a tested, parsimonious preparation-and-evaluation pipeline for observed n1(+15), with a clearly labeled delay stress test. If storage or ages do not help, retain the simpler model and document that limit; the study will not claim a successful predictive improvement without the stated evidence. These experiments and models remain to be implemented and run.

EILF (estimation-identification-localization framework) is a statistical/hydraulic diagnostic framework, not a trained future-pressure regressor. It treats Area C separately and combines Areas A/B because B has no separate inlet-flow meter. The authors observe level-quantization inconsistencies and compute C inflow/demand over 30-minute intervals. For A/B, empirical mode decomposition (EMD) extracts a seasonal consumption component from C, combined with weekly demand patterns to estimate normal flow.\\r\\n3 p Additive seasonal-trend decomposition using LOESS (STL) separates pressure trend, periodic behavior and residuals. Multiscale differences of flow residuals support three-sigma statistical process control and sliding-window incipient-leak assessment. Weekly pressure differences then enter a differential-search hydraulic localization procedure. The 2020 conference proposal [13] mentions VAR, whereas the journal formulation [17] uses STL; these versions must not be treated as one fully specified future-pressure model.\\r\\n4 p <b>Performance and availability.</b> The journal conclusion (p.11) reports 16 detected/localized cases in 2019, TPR=47.83%, FP=5 and EUR167,981. The count of 16 and TPR describe the authors' reported diagnostic results; the conclusion does not supply a common denominator reconciling them. We therefore do not treat 16 as 16 accepted true positives or derive a new recall. These are not pressure-forecast errors. Code is available from the corresponding author on request (Data Availability, p.11); no public repository is supplied. The conference proposal is linked separately in [13]. No request was sent.\\r\\n5 p <b>Consequence for our study.</b> Combining T1, pump flow and AMR information, using a tank balance and decomposing pressure are already prior work. Our candidate contribution must concern how past available information is used for observed n1(+15) and how its added value is evaluated. Any prospective use of EMD/STL or windowed balances must also check time availability; a retrospective decomposition is not automatically an online prediction.\\r\\n6 h 3.8 What transfers, and what must be compared again\\r\\n7 table\\r\\n8 p The detailed rubric audit accompanies this section [21]: task, data/preparation, model, metric/result and code/access evidence are recorded for every case. Published values are retained in their own units and evaluation populations. We have inspected sources and selected code; we have not trained these models or reproduced their reported scores.\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 10 WORDS 446\\r\\n0 h 4.1 Contribution statement and the gap it addresses\\r\\n1 p This section applies \\\"Your Anticipated Contributions\\\" to original-source BattLeDIM research rather than Kaggle notebooks. We propose a reproducible study of recorded n1 pressure at +15 minutes using only six measured signals: n1/n4/n31, T1, PUMP_1 and AMR_n1, plus causal histories and calendar variables. The question is whether storage-informed preparation improves a compact forecaster beyond pressure persistence and matched raw-history models. A secondary experiment asks how explicitly representing reading age changes performance under synthetic auxiliary delays [22].\\r\\n2 p <b>What is already established.</b> ChebNet and SAM-kNN predict future pressures; AGN produces multistep heads including +15; LILA exploits pressure/flow relationships; GATRes studies sparse reconstruction and distribution shifts. EILF already combines tank level, pump flow and AMRs through storage balance [3-11,17,20]. Thus the horizon, selected node, Random Forest, increment target and balance equation are not individually claimed as inventions. Our reviewed sources do not report the matched six-signal, original-SCADA experiment specified here; this is a bounded evidence gap, not proof of worldwide priority.\\r\\n3 table\\r\\n4 h 4.2 Scope and feasible implementation\\r\\n5 p The main experiment uses the original synchronized SCADA, without new hydraulic simulations or extra sensors. Random Forest is the main nonlinear learner because it can test nonlinear effects and interactions in a tabular six-signal design; Ridge supplies an interpretable linear benchmark [23,24]. Graph-network replication, online adaptive memories, an AMR-wide search and distance-based peer ranking are outside this initial contribution. Both peer pressures remain candidates; their value will be measured rather than assumed from pipe distance.\\r\\n6 p The original-source setting changes attribution and reproducibility: organizer data, primary papers and pinned repositories replace Kaggle notebooks. Proposed code and experiments are our future deliverables, distinct from published results. Our label follows recorded pressure, including operating/leak conditions present in the benchmark; it is not a nominal healthy-pressure reference for detecting leaks. No learning model has been fitted for this report. The proposal targets a review-bounded empirical extension; benefits must meet the tests below.\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 11 WORDS 512\\r\\n0 h 4.3 Preparation: a supervised table whose timing can be checked\\r\\n1 p For each five-minute forecast origin u, set y(u)=p_n1(u+15 min). In the primary synchronized experiment, construct 12 readings per selected channel at lags 0,5,...,55 minutes (72 numerical history columns). Add sine/cosine of time of day and seven weekday indicators (nine known calendar columns). Fix this history for all representation comparisons: a storage feature must not silently receive a longer lookback. These are 81 base columns derived from the same six measured channels, not 81 extra sensors.\\r\\n2 p Align the four official tables by unique timestamp; verify spacing, units, duplicates and missingness before creating labels. Retain source time and forecast origin for every input. Never interpolate using future readings, fill a missing response or use leakage annotations as X. Fit Ridge scaling on training rows only; Random Forest requires no standardization. The complete synchronized data need no imputation; if an availability window cannot be built, exclude that origin from all paired variants and report its count.\\r\\n3 p For the secondary synthetic-delay experiment, a reading sampled at t arrives at a_s(t)=t+delta_s(t). At origin u, use the received reading with the greatest source timestamp, tau_s(u)=max{t: a_s(t)<=u}; its age is u-tau_s(u). An older late packet cannot overwrite a newer cached sample. A history entry at u-5j is the cache state as it stood at that earlier origin; later arrivals never retroactively revise it. The target label remains the original recorded n1(+15), not a shifted delayed target.\\r\\n4 h 4.4 Storage features and their physical limits\\r\\n5 p Let L be T1 level, A=pi(16 m)^2/4 the nominal tank area, and w in {15,30} minutes. The storage block S has four features: L(v)-L(v-w) and the following estimated outlet-flow proxy for each w. The pump mean uses the w/5 right-end readings in (v-w,v], in m3/h:\\r\\n6 math\\r\\n7 p For each window choose the freshest endpoint v<=u for which both level endpoints and every required pump reading have arrived. This completeness check matters when packets arrive out of order. Store anchor age u-v. In synchronized data v=u. The balance is inherited from EILF [17]; nominal geometry, rounded level and discrete averaging make it a proxy, not measured sector outflow or total demand. Do not silently clip negative values or subtract all 82 AMRs; the AMR candidate remains only AMR_n1.\\r\\n8 p <b>Why the comparison needs care.</b> With complete histories, these tank features are algebraic transformations of the raw level/flow inputs, not new measurements. For Ridge, linear changes and balance features remain within the raw linear feature span; any change may reflect scaling or regularization. The main representation comparison therefore uses the same Random Forest learner, where an engineered coordinate can change the finite tree fit. Ridge is retained as a redundancy/control check, not evidence of newly created physical information.\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 12 WORDS 464\\r\\n0 h 4.5 Supporting EDA: a hypothesis, not fitted-model performance\\r\\n1 p The daily profiles and correlation matrix in Section 1 remain unchanged. A new past-only check uses January-September 2018 and 78,615 common origins: 78,624 rows minus six initial rows required by the 30-minute change and three final origins whose labels cross into October. It relates backward storage features to the future pressure increment, rather than only to the future pressure level. Source hashes and reproducible calculations accompany the protocol [22].\\r\\n2 table\\r\\n3 h 4.6 Learning models and matched representation tests\\r\\n4 math\\r\\n5 p n1 stays current in the primary and secondary experiments, so persistence predicts r(u). Fit Random Forest to the increment; also fit a direct-pressure Random Forest with the identical inputs and tuning budget to isolate the effect of target parameterization. Ridge is trained on the increment with train-only scaling. Own-n1-history plus calendar models and persistence are essential baselines: strong persistence means a low absolute error alone would not demonstrate a contribution.\\r\\n6 p <b>E1: main synchronized comparison.</b> Compare Random Forest on all six raw histories/calendar (F00) with the same learner plus the four storage features (FS). Use identical origins, histories, targets, split and hyperparameter budget. Separately add signal blocks to own n1/calendar in the order T1, PUMP_1, peers n4/n31, AMR_n1, then remove one block at a time from the full raw model. This distinguishes a useful measurement block from a useful engineered representation; the order is not a claim of physical priority.\\r\\n7 p <b>Model mechanism and budget.</b> Random Forest averages 200 regression trees fitted on bootstrap samples, with squared-error splits; depth and leaf size control complexity. Use max_features=1.0, seed 42 and six configurations: depth {6,10,14} crossed with minimum leaf size {10,40}. Ridge minimizes squared residuals plus alpha times the squared coefficient norm, with alpha {0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000}. Select by October-December 2018 MAE. Keep the primary history at 12 samples; later exploratory changes cannot be chosen from 2019 results [23,24].\\r\\n\\r\\nPAGE 13 WORDS 575\\r\\n0 h 4.7 Secondary extension: unequal delays with a learnable age signal\\r\\n1 p SCADA provides sampling timestamps, not observed receipt times. This is a synthetic robustness experiment. Keep n1 current; for each of n4, n31, T1, PUMP_1 and AMR_n1 draw an independent delay per source day in {0,5,...,L} minutes, independently of pressures and leak labels. Unequal, time-varying delays make reading age vary; a fixed age column in each scenario could not establish a learned age effect. Use the as-of cache construction in Section 4.3, including source-time order and complete balance windows.\\r\\n2 p Train delayed variants on 2018 schedules capped at 15 min (training seed 17; validation 19). All four receive the same cached histories plus the exact raw operands used in S: two level endpoints and w/5 pump samples for each w, adding 13 columns in this secondary experiment. This prevents the storage variant from receiving extra historical readings. Age block A adds five current auxiliary ages and two balance-anchor ages, identically available in FA/FSA. Freeze models, then assess caps 0/5/10/15 with seeds 23/29/31 and coupled draws. Synchronous and delayed training are separate experiments [25].\\r\\n3 table\\r\\n4 h 4.8 Temporal assessment and what counts as an improvement\\r\\n5 p Fit January-September 2018 and select settings on October-December 2018. Purge origins whose +15 labels cross the boundary; carrying earlier predictor history across the boundary is legitimate. Use a common initial 75-minute warm-up, covering the 55-minute history plus maximum 15-minute delay. After selection, refit each chosen model on all eligible 2018 origins and freeze code, data hashes, feature lists, seeds and settings before the new 2019 experiment. Because we have already inspected this public benchmark, call 2019 a final temporal assessment, not an untouched blind test.\\r\\n6 p <b>Measures and decisions.</b> Report MAE (primary) and RMSE in metres, plus skill=1-MAE_model/MAE_persistence. For E1, the primary paired contrast is MAE_FS-MAE_F00; an accuracy claim requires a negative contrast with a 95% block-bootstrap interval below zero and better MAE than persistence and own-history models. For E2, average MAE across nonzero caps and the three schedules; the age-specific primary contrast is FSA-FS. Also report FSA-FA, FSA-F00 and each cap separately. Average paired origin losses over schedules before resampling; schedules are not independent extra years.\\r\\n7 p Use 2,000 moving-block resamples of paired losses with seven-day blocks; test one-/fourteen-day sensitivity. Report counts and MAE/RMSE for filling, draining and near-flat states, using received backward T1 change over 30 min (>0.01/<-0.01/otherwise, m; round numerical differences before thresholding). These are descriptive benchmark intervals, not field-population guarantees. Report RMSE tradeoffs, null/negative results and excluded origins. Operating-state association is not causal attribution.\\r\\n8 p <b>Expected contribution and completion criterion.</b> Deliver the supervised-table builder with timing assertions, reproducible storage/increment models, and a frozen experiment manifest plus paired ablation results.     rel='miniproject/milestone-2/'+name
    with urllib.request.urlopen(base+rel+'?rev='+token,timeout=30) as r:data=r.read()

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

````
